---------- turn 12 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8, F68.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me in here is this memorial I’ve been putting together for my brother. It started out as a simple thing, you know, just honoring him properly, maybe some music he liked, a few photos, people saying a few words. But it’s gotten… bigger. A lot bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days now I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, thinking about trips connected to it, trying to make sure every piece is just right. I’ll start in the morning and then suddenly the whole day’s gone. My wife keeps telling me I’ve pushed aside the garden, my walking group, and just about everything else I used to do. I don’t know, I feel like it matters, but she thinks I’ve gotten too wrapped up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial has to be perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly, but it does feel like every piece has to be right, if that makes sense. It started as just a memorial for my brother, something simple and decent, but then I keep thinking of one more song he loved, one more person I should call, one more photo or display that would explain who he was better.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, maybe there should be a trip connected to it, or some place that meant something to him, and suddenly I’m spending the whole day on it. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been keeping up with the walking group or much of anything else. I don’t mean for it to take over like that, but once I get started, it feels important to keep pushing until it all comes together the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you think about not being able to include every detail you want in the memorial?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe uncomfortable, yes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from just feeling like I’m not doing right by him. If I think, “Well, leave that song out,” or “Maybe don’t call that person,” I start going over it in my head, like maybe that was the one thing that would’ve mattered, or the one person who should’ve been included.\n\nBut I don’t know if it’s fear the way you mean it. It’s more like… I keep thinking there’s a better version of it, and if I stop too soon then I’ll have missed something important. My wife says I’m making too much of every little detail, and maybe I am, but when it’s my brother, it doesn’t feel little to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of every aspect of the memorial?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I need to be in charge of every single part. I mean, people can help, and my wife has tried, and I’m not against that in principle. It’s just that when someone else suggests something, I start thinking, well, they didn’t know him the way I did, or they might miss why a certain song matters, or why a certain photo should go next to another one.\n\nSo I guess I do end up holding onto a lot of it myself. Not because I’m trying to boss everyone around, at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel like I’m the one who understands what it’s supposed to mean. But then again, maybe that does come across like I have to run the whole thing. My wife would probably say yes to that question faster than I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if the memorial wasn't done exactly as you envision it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know how it sounds when I say it out loud, but yes, in a way I do. Not like the chairs being crooked would make the roof fall in or anything like that. It’s more… there are things in those old ballads my brother kept, the ones he loved, and I’ve listened to them over and over now. I’m certain there are warnings tucked into the words. Not just lyrics, not just old sad songs.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, that I’m hearing what I want to hear because I miss him, but I know what I heard. Certain lines stand out, like they’re meant for me, like they’re telling me not to leave something out or not to put things in the wrong order. So if the memorial isn’t done the way it’s supposed to be done, the way I’m seeing it in my head, then yes, I do worry something bad could come from ignoring that. I can’t prove it to you neatly, I know that, but it feels very clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the ballads if they listened to them the way you have?\n\nPatient: I think they could, if they really listened. Not just had it playing in the background while they were washing dishes or something, but sat with it the way I have, listened to the words, the pauses, the way certain lines come back around. I know that probably sounds strange, but I’m not just making it up out of thin air.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and maybe other people would say the same thing at first. But I know what I heard. Those old ballads my brother kept, they’re not just songs to me now. There are warnings tucked into them, like certain words are meant to point me toward what I’m supposed to do for him. Maybe not everyone would catch it right away, because they didn’t know him and they haven’t listened over and over like I have. But if they listened the right way, I think they’d hear at least some of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these warnings in the ballads are meant for anyone else besides you?\n\nPatient: Mostly me. That’s the honest answer. I don’t think they’re, you know, broadcasting to everyone like some announcement on the radio. It feels more particular than that. They were my brother’s ballads, the ones he kept, the ones he went back to, and now when I listen to them, certain lines don’t just sound like lyrics anymore. They feel aimed at me.\n\nI know that sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would shake her head and say I’m reading too much into it again. But I still know what I heard in those words. It’s like there are warnings tucked inside them, and they’re warning me about what I’m supposed to do, how I’m supposed to handle the memorial, what not to leave out.\n\nMaybe someone else could understand parts of it if they were close enough to him, or if they listened carefully enough, but I don’t think it’s really meant for just anybody. It feels like it was left for me to catch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could be wrong about the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: I suppose anybody could be wrong about anything, if you put it that way. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect judge of every little thing. I know grief can do things to a person, and I know my wife thinks that’s what this is, that I’m taking these old songs my brother kept and turning them into something they’re not.\n\nBut when I actually sit there and listen—really listen—it doesn’t feel like a mistake. It feels very clear. Those ballads aren’t just songs to me now. There are warnings tucked into them, certain words and lines that stand out like they’re meant for me. And even when I try to tell myself, “Maybe she’s right, maybe you’re reading too much into it,” I still come back to the same thing: I know what I heard.\n\nSo could I be wrong? I guess, in a plain common-sense way, yes, maybe. But I don’t feel wrong about it. I feel certain enough that I can’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider changing your plans for the memorial if someone you trust, like a close friend or family member, listened to the ballads and didn't hear the warnings you do?\n\nPatient: I’d listen to them, yes. I mean, if it was someone I really trusted, I wouldn’t just brush them off. If my wife sat down with me, or someone who knew my brother well, and they said, “I don’t hear what you’re hearing,” I’d try to take that in.\n\nBut changing the memorial plans… that’s harder. Because it isn’t just that I’m hearing a sad song and making it fit. I know it sounds odd when I say it out loud, but those old ballads he kept, they don’t feel like ordinary songs anymore. There are words in them, certain lines, that land in a way that feels like a warning. Like they’re meant for me to notice. And my wife has already told me I’m reading too much into it, more than once, but that doesn’t make what I heard go away.\n\nSo if someone else didn’t hear it, I might think, well, maybe they’re not listening the same way, or maybe they don’t have the same connection to him. I don’t mean that in an arrogant way. It’s just… I’ve spent time with those songs. I’ve heard the warnings in them. I know what I heard.\n\nI might make small adjustments if people were upset or if they thought something would embarrass the family, but I don’t think I could just ignore the warnings altogether. Not just because someone else didn’t pick up on them. That would feel like turning my back on something I was supposed to understand.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe you hear warnings in the ballads if everyone you trust told you they don't hear them?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I don’t say that to be stubborn, and I don’t mean I wouldn’t care what they thought. If everyone I trusted sat me down and said, “We don’t hear any warnings in those songs,” that would shake me. I’d probably feel embarrassed, maybe defensive, and I’d wonder if I was somehow hearing it differently because it was my brother and because those were his old ballads.\n\nBut even then, I don’t think it would make me stop believing it. Not completely. Because when I listen to them, it isn’t vague to me. It’s not just a mood or me saying, “Oh, this song reminds me of him.” Certain words catch, certain lines feel placed there, almost tucked inside the music for me to notice. I know it sounds odd. I can hear that. But I’m certain those old ballads he kept aren’t just songs now. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nMy wife has already told me I’m reading too much into it, and I’ve tried to take that seriously. I really have. But her saying that doesn’t change what I heard in those words. So if everyone said the same thing, I might get quieter about it, maybe keep more of it to myself, but inside I think I’d still know what I heard.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings you hear in the ballads could be a product of your own mind rather than actual messages?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to ask myself that, honestly. I’m not completely closed off to the idea that my mind could be… shaping things, I suppose. I know after losing my brother, and with everything tied up in those old ballads he kept, a person can start noticing things they might not have noticed before.\n\nBut when you ask if it could just be coming from my own mind, that’s where I get stuck. Because when I listen to them, it doesn’t feel like I’m inventing it. It feels like the warnings are already there, tucked into the words, and I’m just finally hearing them properly. Certain lines stand out in a way I can’t explain away. It’s not like I sat there wanting to find a message and then forced one into the song.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I understand why she says that. If I were listening to someone else say this, maybe I’d think it sounded strange too. But knowing it sounds odd doesn’t make it feel any less real to me. I still know what I heard in those words. So could my mind be part of it? Maybe, in some way. But I don’t believe it’s only my mind. I’m certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the warnings you hear in the ballads are not real?\n\nPatient: I suppose if I’m being fair, I can say there’s a possibility. I mean, I know how it sounds when I describe it. I’m not deaf to that. If someone else told me, “There are warnings hidden in these old ballads,” I might wonder if grief was making them hear things in a certain way.\n\nBut for me, sitting there with those songs my brother kept, listening to the words over and over… it doesn’t feel unreal. It doesn’t feel like I’m making it up. Certain lines just hit differently, like they’re not only lyrics anymore, like they were meant to be noticed by me. That’s the part I can’t get away from.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I try to respect that because she’s not trying to hurt me. She’s worried, I think. But even when she says that, I still know what I heard in those words. So yes, maybe there’s some small possibility I’m wrong, but I don’t really believe the warnings are not real. I’m still pretty certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked inside them for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F42.2", "F43.8", "F68.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I’m not completely closed off to the idea that my mind could be… shaping things, I suppose. I know after losing my brother, and with everything tied up in those old ballads he kept, a person can start noticing things they might not have noticed before.\n\nBut when you ask if it could just be coming from my own mind, that’s where I get stuck. Because when I listen to them, it doesn’t feel like I’m inventing it. It feels like the warnings are already there, tucked into the words, and I’m just finally hearing them properly. If I were listening to someone else say this, maybe I’d think it sounded strange too. But knowing it sounds odd doesn’t make it feel any less real to me. I still know what I heard in those words. So could my mind be part of it? Maybe, in some way. But I don’t believe it’s only my mind. I’m certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the warnings you hear in the ballads are not real?\n\nPatient: I suppose if I’m being fair, I can say there’s a possibility. Yes… I think they would. That’s the strange part, I suppose. It doesn’t feel like the warnings only exist because I happen to be listening at that moment, like I’m creating them while the song plays. It feels more like they’re already in there, tucked into the ballads, whether I have them on or not.

I know that probably sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would say, “You’re reading too much into it.” She has said that. But to me, those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs anymore. The words are carrying something. When I listen, I’m not putting the warning there — I’m noticing what’s already there.

So if I wasn’t listening, I suppose I wouldn’t be hearing them right then, obviously. But that doesn’t mean the warnings disappear. They’d still be in the songs, waiting, in a way. And when I go back and play them again, certain lines catch the same way, like they were meant for me to hear. That’s why it’s hard for me to dismiss it. I still know what I heard in those words.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8, F68.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me in here is this memorial I’ve been putting together for my brother. It started out as a simple thing, you know, just honoring him properly, maybe some music he liked, a few photos, people saying a few words. But it’s gotten… bigger. A lot bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days now I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, thinking about trips connected to it, trying to make sure every piece is just right. I’ll start in the morning and then suddenly the whole day’s gone. My wife keeps telling me I’ve pushed aside the garden, my walking group, and just about everything else I used to do. I don’t know, I feel like it matters, but she thinks I’ve gotten too wrapped up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial has to be perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly, but it does feel like every piece has to be right, if that makes sense. It started as just a memorial for my brother, something simple and decent, but then I keep thinking of one more song he loved, one more person I should call, one more photo or display that would explain who he was better.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, maybe there should be a trip connected to it, or some place that meant something to him, and suddenly I’m spending the whole day on it. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been keeping up with the walking group or much of anything else. I don’t mean for it to take over like that, but once I get started, it feels important to keep pushing until it all comes together the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you think about not being able to include every detail you want in the memorial?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe uncomfortable, yes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from just feeling like I’m not doing right by him. If I think, “Well, leave that song out,” or “Maybe don’t call that person,” I start going over it in my head, like maybe that was the one thing that would’ve mattered, or the one person who should’ve been included.\n\nBut I don’t know if it’s fear the way you mean it. It’s more like… I keep thinking there’s a better version of it, and if I stop too soon then I’ll have missed something important. My wife says I’m making too much of every little detail, and maybe I am, but when it’s my brother, it doesn’t feel little to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of every aspect of the memorial?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I need to be in charge of every single part. I mean, people can help, and my wife has tried, and I’m not against that in principle. It’s just that when someone else suggests something, I start thinking, well, they didn’t know him the way I did, or they might miss why a certain song matters, or why a certain photo should go next to another one.\n\nSo I guess I do end up holding onto a lot of it myself. Not because I’m trying to boss everyone around, at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel like I’m the one who understands what it’s supposed to mean. But then again, maybe that does come across like I have to run the whole thing. My wife would probably say yes to that question faster than I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if the memorial wasn't done exactly as you envision it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know how it sounds when I say it out loud, but yes, in a way I do. Not like the chairs being crooked would make the roof fall in or anything like that. It’s more… there are things in those old ballads my brother kept, the ones he loved, and I’ve listened to them over and over now. I’m certain there are warnings tucked into the words. Not just lyrics, not just old sad songs.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, that I’m hearing what I want to hear because I miss him, but I know what I heard. Certain lines stand out, like they’re meant for me, like they’re telling me not to leave something out or not to put things in the wrong order. So if the memorial isn’t done the way it’s supposed to be done, the way I’m seeing it in my head, then yes, I do worry something bad could come from ignoring that. I can’t prove it to you neatly, I know that, but it feels very clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the ballads if they listened to them the way you have?\n\nPatient: I think they could, if they really listened. Not just had it playing in the background while they were washing dishes or something, but sat with it the way I have, listened to the words, the pauses, the way certain lines come back around. I know that probably sounds strange, but I’m not just making it up out of thin air.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and maybe other people would say the same thing at first. But I know what I heard. Those old ballads my brother kept, they’re not just songs to me now. There are warnings tucked into them, like certain words are meant to point me toward what I’m supposed to do for him. Maybe not everyone would catch it right away, because they didn’t know him and they haven’t listened over and over like I have. But if they listened the right way, I think they’d hear at least some of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these warnings in the ballads are meant for anyone else besides you?\n\nPatient: Mostly me. That’s the honest answer. I don’t think they’re, you know, broadcasting to everyone like some announcement on the radio. It feels more particular than that. They were my brother’s ballads, the ones he kept, the ones he went back to, and now when I listen to them, certain lines don’t just sound like lyrics anymore. They feel aimed at me.\n\nI know that sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would shake her head and say I’m reading too much into it again. But I still know what I heard in those words. It’s like there are warnings tucked inside them, and they’re warning me about what I’m supposed to do, how I’m supposed to handle the memorial, what not to leave out.\n\nMaybe someone else could understand parts of it if they were close enough to him, or if they listened carefully enough, but I don’t think it’s really meant for just anybody. It feels like it was left for me to catch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could be wrong about the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: I suppose anybody could be wrong about anything, if you put it that way. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect judge of every little thing. I know grief can do things to a person, and I know my wife thinks that’s what this is, that I’m taking these old songs my brother kept and turning them into something they’re not.\n\nBut when I actually sit there and listen—really listen—it doesn’t feel like a mistake. It feels very clear. Those ballads aren’t just songs to me now. There are warnings tucked into them, certain words and lines that stand out like they’re meant for me. And even when I try to tell myself, “Maybe she’s right, maybe you’re reading too much into it,” I still come back to the same thing: I know what I heard.\n\nSo could I be wrong? I guess, in a plain common-sense way, yes, maybe. But I don’t feel wrong about it. I feel certain enough that I can’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider changing your plans for the memorial if someone you trust, like a close friend or family member, listened to the ballads and didn't hear the warnings you do?\n\nPatient: I’d listen to them, yes. I mean, if it was someone I really trusted, I wouldn’t just brush them off. If my wife sat down with me, or someone who knew my brother well, and they said, “I don’t hear what you’re hearing,” I’d try to take that in.\n\nBut changing the memorial plans… that’s harder. Because it isn’t just that I’m hearing a sad song and making it fit. I know it sounds odd when I say it out loud, but those old ballads he kept, they don’t feel like ordinary songs anymore. There are words in them, certain lines, that land in a way that feels like a warning. Like they’re meant for me to notice. And my wife has already told me I’m reading too much into it, more than once, but that doesn’t make what I heard go away.\n\nSo if someone else didn’t hear it, I might think, well, maybe they’re not listening the same way, or maybe they don’t have the same connection to him. I don’t mean that in an arrogant way. It’s just… I’ve spent time with those songs. I’ve heard the warnings in them. I know what I heard.\n\nI might make small adjustments if people were upset or if they thought something would embarrass the family, but I don’t think I could just ignore the warnings altogether. Not just because someone else didn’t pick up on them. That would feel like turning my back on something I was supposed to understand.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe you hear warnings in the ballads if everyone you trust told you they don't hear them?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I don’t say that to be stubborn, and I don’t mean I wouldn’t care what they thought. If everyone I trusted sat me down and said, “We don’t hear any warnings in those songs,” that would shake me. I’d probably feel embarrassed, maybe defensive, and I’d wonder if I was somehow hearing it differently because it was my brother and because those were his old ballads.\n\nBut even then, I don’t think it would make me stop believing it. Not completely. Because when I listen to them, it isn’t vague to me. It’s not just a mood or me saying, “Oh, this song reminds me of him.” Certain words catch, certain lines feel placed there, almost tucked inside the music for me to notice. I know it sounds odd. I can hear that. But I’m certain those old ballads he kept aren’t just songs now. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nMy wife has already told me I’m reading too much into it, and I’ve tried to take that seriously. I really have. But her saying that doesn’t change what I heard in those words. So if everyone said the same thing, I might get quieter about it, maybe keep more of it to myself, but inside I think I’d still know what I heard.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings you hear in the ballads could be a product of your own mind rather than actual messages?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to ask myself that, honestly. I’m not completely closed off to the idea that my mind could be… shaping things, I suppose. I know after losing my brother, and with everything tied up in those old ballads he kept, a person can start noticing things they might not have noticed before.\n\nBut when you ask if it could just be coming from my own mind, that’s where I get stuck. Because when I listen to them, it doesn’t feel like I’m inventing it. It feels like the warnings are already there, tucked into the words, and I’m just finally hearing them properly. Certain lines stand out in a way I can’t explain away. It’s not like I sat there wanting to find a message and then forced one into the song.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I understand why she says that. If I were listening to someone else say this, maybe I’d think it sounded strange too. But knowing it sounds odd doesn’t make it feel any less real to me. I still know what I heard in those words. So could my mind be part of it? Maybe, in some way. But I don’t believe it’s only my mind. I’m certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the warnings you hear in the ballads are not real?\n\nPatient: I suppose if I’m being fair, I can say there’s a possibility. I mean, I know how it sounds when I describe it. I’m not deaf to that. If someone else told me, “There are warnings hidden in these old ballads,” I might wonder if grief was making them hear things in a certain way.\n\nBut for me, sitting there with those songs my brother kept, listening to the words over and over… it doesn’t feel unreal. It doesn’t feel like I’m making it up. Certain lines just hit differently, like they’re not only lyrics anymore, like they were meant to be noticed by me. That’s the part I can’t get away from.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I try to respect that because she’s not trying to hurt me. She’s worried, I think. But even when she says that, I still know what I heard in those words. So yes, maybe there’s some small possibility I’m wrong, but I don’t really believe the warnings are not real. I’m still pretty certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked inside them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings in the ballads would still be there if you weren't listening to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they would. That’s the strange part, I suppose. It doesn’t feel like the warnings only exist because I happen to be listening at that moment, like I’m creating them while the song plays. It feels more like they’re already in there, tucked into the ballads, whether I have them on or not.\n\nI know that probably sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would say, “You’re reading too much into it.” She has said that. But to me, those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs anymore. The words are carrying something. When I listen, I’m not putting the warning there — I’m noticing what’s already there.\n\nSo if I wasn’t listening, I suppose I wouldn’t be hearing them right then, obviously. But that doesn’t mean the warnings disappear. They’d still be in the songs, waiting, in a way. And when I go back and play them again, certain lines catch the same way, like they were meant for me to hear. That’s why it’s hard for me to dismiss it. I still know what I heard in those words.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, people can help, and my wife has tried, and I’m not against that in principle. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings you hear in the ballads could be a product of your own mind rather than actual messages?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to ask myself that, honestly. I’m not completely closed off to the idea that my mind could be… shaping things, I suppose. I know after losing my brother, and with everything tied up in those old ballads he kept, a person can start noticing things they might not have noticed before.\n\nBut when you ask if it could just be coming from my own mind, that’s where I get stuck. Because when I listen to them, it doesn’t feel like I’m inventing it. It feels like the warnings are already there, tucked into the words, and I’m just finally hearing them properly. Certain lines stand out in a way I can’t explain away. It’s not like I sat there wanting to find a message and then forced one into the song.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I understand why she says that. If I were listening to someone else say this, maybe I’d think it sounded strange too. But knowing it sounds odd doesn’t make it feel any less real to me. I still know what I heard in those words. So could my mind be part of it? Maybe, in some way. But I don’t believe it’s only my mind. I’m certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the warnings you hear in the ballads are not real?\n\nPatient: I suppose if I’m being fair, I can say there’s a possibility. I mean, I know how it sounds when I describe it. I’m not deaf to that. If someone else told me, “There are warnings hidden in these old ballads,” I might wonder if grief was making them hear things in a certain way.\n\nBut for me, sitting there with those songs my brother kept, listening to the words over and over… it doesn’t feel unreal. It doesn’t feel like I’m making it up. Certain lines just hit differently, like they’re not only lyrics anymore, like they were meant to be noticed by me. That’s the part I can’t get away from.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I try to respect that because she’s not trying to hurt me. She’s worried, I think. But even when she says that, I still know what I heard in those words. So yes, maybe there’s some small possibility I’m wrong, but I don’t really believe the warnings are not real. I’m still pretty certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked inside them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings in the ballads would still be there if you weren't listening to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they would. That’s the strange part, I suppose. It doesn’t feel like the warnings only exist because I happen to be listening at that moment, like I’m creating them while the song plays. It feels more like they’re already in there, tucked into the ballads, whether I have them on or not.\n\nI know that probably sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would say, “You’re reading too much into it.” She has said that. But to me, those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs anymore. The words are carrying something. When I listen, I’m not putting the warning there — I’m noticing what’s already there.\n\nSo if I wasn’t listening, I suppose I wouldn’t be hearing them right then, obviously. But that doesn’t mean the warnings disappear. They’d still be in the songs, waiting, in a way. And when I go back and play them again, certain lines catch the same way, like they were meant for me to hear. That’s why it’s hard for me to dismiss it. I still know what I heard in those words.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the warnings in the ballads would still make sense to you if your brother was alive and told you there were no hidden messages in them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know it sounds odd, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me, and when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I still know what I heard in those words.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If my brother were alive and he sat right in front of me and said, “No, there are no hidden messages in those songs,” I’d want to believe him. I really would. He knew those ballads better than anyone, and they were his, in a way. I’d listen to him. I wouldn’t just brush him off.

But… I don’t know that it would make the warnings stop making sense to me. That’s the hard part. Because what I hear in them doesn’t feel like something I decided on because he died, or because someone told me to look for it. It feels like the words themselves are carrying it. Like the warnings are tucked in there, whether anyone else admits it or not.

Maybe I’d think he was trying to protect me, or maybe he didn’t hear them the same way I do. I know that sounds odd. I can hear that it sounds odd even as I’m saying it. My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and maybe my brother would say the same thing if he were here. But even then… I’d still know what I heard in those words.

So yes, I suppose it would shake me some if he said that. I’d be upset, probably confused. But I don’t think it would make me believe the ballads are just songs. Not completely. I’m certain there are warnings in them for me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F43.8, F68.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally got me in here is this memorial I’ve been putting together for my brother. It started out as a simple thing, you know, just honoring him properly, maybe some music he liked, a few photos, people saying a few words. But it’s gotten… bigger. A lot bigger than I meant it to.\n\nMost days now I’m chasing down songs, calling people, figuring out displays, thinking about trips connected to it, trying to make sure every piece is just right. I’ll start in the morning and then suddenly the whole day’s gone. My wife keeps telling me I’ve pushed aside the garden, my walking group, and just about everything else I used to do. I don’t know, I feel like it matters, but she thinks I’ve gotten too wrapped up in it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the memorial has to be perfect?\n\nPatient: Perfect… I don’t know if I’d use that word exactly, but it does feel like every piece has to be right, if that makes sense. It started as just a memorial for my brother, something simple and decent, but then I keep thinking of one more song he loved, one more person I should call, one more photo or display that would explain who he was better.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, maybe there should be a trip connected to it, or some place that meant something to him, and suddenly I’m spending the whole day on it. My wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been keeping up with the walking group or much of anything else. I don’t mean for it to take over like that, but once I get started, it feels important to keep pushing until it all comes together the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you think about not being able to include every detail you want in the memorial?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe uncomfortable, yes, but it’s hard for me to separate that from just feeling like I’m not doing right by him. If I think, “Well, leave that song out,” or “Maybe don’t call that person,” I start going over it in my head, like maybe that was the one thing that would’ve mattered, or the one person who should’ve been included.\n\nBut I don’t know if it’s fear the way you mean it. It’s more like… I keep thinking there’s a better version of it, and if I stop too soon then I’ll have missed something important. My wife says I’m making too much of every little detail, and maybe I am, but when it’s my brother, it doesn’t feel little to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of every aspect of the memorial?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I need to be in charge of every single part. I mean, people can help, and my wife has tried, and I’m not against that in principle. It’s just that when someone else suggests something, I start thinking, well, they didn’t know him the way I did, or they might miss why a certain song matters, or why a certain photo should go next to another one.\n\nSo I guess I do end up holding onto a lot of it myself. Not because I’m trying to boss everyone around, at least I don’t think so. It’s more that I feel like I’m the one who understands what it’s supposed to mean. But then again, maybe that does come across like I have to run the whole thing. My wife would probably say yes to that question faster than I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if the memorial wasn't done exactly as you envision it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know how it sounds when I say it out loud, but yes, in a way I do. Not like the chairs being crooked would make the roof fall in or anything like that. It’s more… there are things in those old ballads my brother kept, the ones he loved, and I’ve listened to them over and over now. I’m certain there are warnings tucked into the words. Not just lyrics, not just old sad songs.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, that I’m hearing what I want to hear because I miss him, but I know what I heard. Certain lines stand out, like they’re meant for me, like they’re telling me not to leave something out or not to put things in the wrong order. So if the memorial isn’t done the way it’s supposed to be done, the way I’m seeing it in my head, then yes, I do worry something bad could come from ignoring that. I can’t prove it to you neatly, I know that, but it feels very clear to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people would hear those warnings in the ballads if they listened to them the way you have?\n\nPatient: I think they could, if they really listened. Not just had it playing in the background while they were washing dishes or something, but sat with it the way I have, listened to the words, the pauses, the way certain lines come back around. I know that probably sounds strange, but I’m not just making it up out of thin air.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and maybe other people would say the same thing at first. But I know what I heard. Those old ballads my brother kept, they’re not just songs to me now. There are warnings tucked into them, like certain words are meant to point me toward what I’m supposed to do for him. Maybe not everyone would catch it right away, because they didn’t know him and they haven’t listened over and over like I have. But if they listened the right way, I think they’d hear at least some of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these warnings in the ballads are meant for anyone else besides you?\n\nPatient: Mostly me. That’s the honest answer. I don’t think they’re, you know, broadcasting to everyone like some announcement on the radio. It feels more particular than that. They were my brother’s ballads, the ones he kept, the ones he went back to, and now when I listen to them, certain lines don’t just sound like lyrics anymore. They feel aimed at me.\n\nI know that sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would shake her head and say I’m reading too much into it again. But I still know what I heard in those words. It’s like there are warnings tucked inside them, and they’re warning me about what I’m supposed to do, how I’m supposed to handle the memorial, what not to leave out.\n\nMaybe someone else could understand parts of it if they were close enough to him, or if they listened carefully enough, but I don’t think it’s really meant for just anybody. It feels like it was left for me to catch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could be wrong about the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: I suppose anybody could be wrong about anything, if you put it that way. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect judge of every little thing. I know grief can do things to a person, and I know my wife thinks that’s what this is, that I’m taking these old songs my brother kept and turning them into something they’re not.\n\nBut when I actually sit there and listen—really listen—it doesn’t feel like a mistake. It feels very clear. Those ballads aren’t just songs to me now. There are warnings tucked into them, certain words and lines that stand out like they’re meant for me. And even when I try to tell myself, “Maybe she’s right, maybe you’re reading too much into it,” I still come back to the same thing: I know what I heard.\n\nSo could I be wrong? I guess, in a plain common-sense way, yes, maybe. But I don’t feel wrong about it. I feel certain enough that I can’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider changing your plans for the memorial if someone you trust, like a close friend or family member, listened to the ballads and didn't hear the warnings you do?\n\nPatient: I’d listen to them, yes. I mean, if it was someone I really trusted, I wouldn’t just brush them off. If my wife sat down with me, or someone who knew my brother well, and they said, “I don’t hear what you’re hearing,” I’d try to take that in.\n\nBut changing the memorial plans… that’s harder. Because it isn’t just that I’m hearing a sad song and making it fit. I know it sounds odd when I say it out loud, but those old ballads he kept, they don’t feel like ordinary songs anymore. There are words in them, certain lines, that land in a way that feels like a warning. Like they’re meant for me to notice. And my wife has already told me I’m reading too much into it, more than once, but that doesn’t make what I heard go away.\n\nSo if someone else didn’t hear it, I might think, well, maybe they’re not listening the same way, or maybe they don’t have the same connection to him. I don’t mean that in an arrogant way. It’s just… I’ve spent time with those songs. I’ve heard the warnings in them. I know what I heard.\n\nI might make small adjustments if people were upset or if they thought something would embarrass the family, but I don’t think I could just ignore the warnings altogether. Not just because someone else didn’t pick up on them. That would feel like turning my back on something I was supposed to understand.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe you hear warnings in the ballads if everyone you trust told you they don't hear them?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I don’t say that to be stubborn, and I don’t mean I wouldn’t care what they thought. If everyone I trusted sat me down and said, “We don’t hear any warnings in those songs,” that would shake me. I’d probably feel embarrassed, maybe defensive, and I’d wonder if I was somehow hearing it differently because it was my brother and because those were his old ballads.\n\nBut even then, I don’t think it would make me stop believing it. Not completely. Because when I listen to them, it isn’t vague to me. It’s not just a mood or me saying, “Oh, this song reminds me of him.” Certain words catch, certain lines feel placed there, almost tucked inside the music for me to notice. I know it sounds odd. I can hear that. But I’m certain those old ballads he kept aren’t just songs now. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nMy wife has already told me I’m reading too much into it, and I’ve tried to take that seriously. I really have. But her saying that doesn’t change what I heard in those words. So if everyone said the same thing, I might get quieter about it, maybe keep more of it to myself, but inside I think I’d still know what I heard.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings you hear in the ballads could be a product of your own mind rather than actual messages?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to ask myself that, honestly. I’m not completely closed off to the idea that my mind could be… shaping things, I suppose. I know after losing my brother, and with everything tied up in those old ballads he kept, a person can start noticing things they might not have noticed before.\n\nBut when you ask if it could just be coming from my own mind, that’s where I get stuck. Because when I listen to them, it doesn’t feel like I’m inventing it. It feels like the warnings are already there, tucked into the words, and I’m just finally hearing them properly. Certain lines stand out in a way I can’t explain away. It’s not like I sat there wanting to find a message and then forced one into the song.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I understand why she says that. If I were listening to someone else say this, maybe I’d think it sounded strange too. But knowing it sounds odd doesn’t make it feel any less real to me. I still know what I heard in those words. So could my mind be part of it? Maybe, in some way. But I don’t believe it’s only my mind. I’m certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the warnings you hear in the ballads are not real?\n\nPatient: I suppose if I’m being fair, I can say there’s a possibility. I mean, I know how it sounds when I describe it. I’m not deaf to that. If someone else told me, “There are warnings hidden in these old ballads,” I might wonder if grief was making them hear things in a certain way.\n\nBut for me, sitting there with those songs my brother kept, listening to the words over and over… it doesn’t feel unreal. It doesn’t feel like I’m making it up. Certain lines just hit differently, like they’re not only lyrics anymore, like they were meant to be noticed by me. That’s the part I can’t get away from.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I try to respect that because she’s not trying to hurt me. She’s worried, I think. But even when she says that, I still know what I heard in those words. So yes, maybe there’s some small possibility I’m wrong, but I don’t really believe the warnings are not real. I’m still pretty certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked inside them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings in the ballads would still be there if you weren't listening to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they would. That’s the strange part, I suppose. It doesn’t feel like the warnings only exist because I happen to be listening at that moment, like I’m creating them while the song plays. It feels more like they’re already in there, tucked into the ballads, whether I have them on or not.\n\nI know that probably sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would say, “You’re reading too much into it.” She has said that. But to me, those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs anymore. The words are carrying something. When I listen, I’m not putting the warning there — I’m noticing what’s already there.\n\nSo if I wasn’t listening, I suppose I wouldn’t be hearing them right then, obviously. But that doesn’t mean the warnings disappear. They’d still be in the songs, waiting, in a way. And when I go back and play them again, certain lines catch the same way, like they were meant for me to hear. That’s why it’s hard for me to dismiss it. I still know what I heard in those words.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings in the ballads would still make sense to you if your brother was alive and told you there were no hidden messages in them?\n\nPatient: If my brother were alive and he sat right in front of me and said, “No, there are no hidden messages in those songs,” I’d want to believe him. I really would. He knew those ballads better than anyone, and they were his, in a way. I’d listen to him. I wouldn’t just brush him off.\n\nBut… I don’t know that it would make the warnings stop making sense to me. That’s the hard part. Because what I hear in them doesn’t feel like something I decided on because he died, or because someone told me to look for it. It feels like the words themselves are carrying it. Like the warnings are tucked in there, whether anyone else admits it or not.\n\nMaybe I’d think he was trying to protect me, or maybe he didn’t hear them the same way I do. I know that sounds odd. I can hear that it sounds odd even as I’m saying it. My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and maybe my brother would say the same thing if he were here. But even then… I’d still know what I heard in those words.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it would shake me some if he said that. I’d be upset, probably confused. But I don’t think it would make me believe the ballads are just songs. Not completely. I’m certain there are warnings in them for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

That’s the honest answer. I don’t think they’re, you know, broadcasting to everyone like some announcement on the radio. It feels more particular than that. They were my brother’s ballads, the ones he kept, the ones he went back to, and now when I listen to them, certain lines don’t just sound like lyrics anymore. They feel aimed at me.\n\nI know that sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would shake her head and say I’m reading too much into it again. But I still know what I heard in those words. It’s like there are warnings tucked inside them, and they’re warning me about what I’m supposed to do, how I’m supposed to handle the memorial, what not to leave out.\n\nMaybe someone else could understand parts of it if they were close enough to him, or if they listened carefully enough, but I don’t think it’s really meant for just anybody. It feels like it was left for me to catch.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could be wrong about the warnings you hear in the ballads?\n\nPatient: I suppose anybody could be wrong about anything, if you put it that way. I’m not trying to say I’m some perfect judge of every little thing. I know grief can do things to a person, and I know my wife thinks that’s what this is, that I’m taking these old songs my brother kept and turning them into something they’re not.\n\nBut when I actually sit there and listen—really listen—it doesn’t feel like a mistake. It feels very clear. Those ballads aren’t just songs to me now. There are warnings tucked into them, certain words and lines that stand out like they’re meant for me. And even when I try to tell myself, “Maybe she’s right, maybe you’re reading too much into it,” I still come back to the same thing: I know what I heard.\n\nSo could I be wrong? I guess, in a plain common-sense way, yes, maybe. But I don’t feel wrong about it. I feel certain enough that I can’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider changing your plans for the memorial if someone you trust, like a close friend or family member, listened to the ballads and didn't hear the warnings you do?\n\nPatient: I’d listen to them, yes. I mean, if it was someone I really trusted, I wouldn’t just brush them off. If my wife sat down with me, or someone who knew my brother well, and they said, “I don’t hear what you’re hearing,” I’d try to take that in.\n\nBut changing the memorial plans… that’s harder. Because it isn’t just that I’m hearing a sad song and making it fit. I know it sounds odd when I say it out loud, but those old ballads he kept, they don’t feel like ordinary songs anymore. There are words in them, certain lines, that land in a way that feels like a warning. Like they’re meant for me to notice. And my wife has already told me I’m reading too much into it, more than once, but that doesn’t make what I heard go away.\n\nSo if someone else didn’t hear it, I might think, well, maybe they’re not listening the same way, or maybe they don’t have the same connection to him. I don’t mean that in an arrogant way. It’s just… I’ve spent time with those songs. I’ve heard the warnings in them. I know what I heard.\n\nI might make small adjustments if people were upset or if they thought something would embarrass the family, but I don’t think I could just ignore the warnings altogether. Not just because someone else didn’t pick up on them. That would feel like turning my back on something I was supposed to understand.\n\nDoctor: Would you still believe you hear warnings in the ballads if everyone you trust told you they don't hear them?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I don’t say that to be stubborn, and I don’t mean I wouldn’t care what they thought. If everyone I trusted sat me down and said, “We don’t hear any warnings in those songs,” that would shake me. I’d probably feel embarrassed, maybe defensive, and I’d wonder if I was somehow hearing it differently because it was my brother and because those were his old ballads.\n\nBut even then, I don’t think it would make me stop believing it. Not completely. Because when I listen to them, it isn’t vague to me. It’s not just a mood or me saying, “Oh, this song reminds me of him.” Certain words catch, certain lines feel placed there, almost tucked inside the music for me to notice. I know it sounds odd. I can hear that. But I’m certain those old ballads he kept aren’t just songs now. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nMy wife has already told me I’m reading too much into it, and I’ve tried to take that seriously. I really have. But her saying that doesn’t change what I heard in those words. So if everyone said the same thing, I might get quieter about it, maybe keep more of it to myself, but inside I think I’d still know what I heard.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings you hear in the ballads could be a product of your own mind rather than actual messages?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to ask myself that, honestly. I’m not completely closed off to the idea that my mind could be… shaping things, I suppose. I know after losing my brother, and with everything tied up in those old ballads he kept, a person can start noticing things they might not have noticed before.\n\nBut when you ask if it could just be coming from my own mind, that’s where I get stuck. Because when I listen to them, it doesn’t feel like I’m inventing it. It feels like the warnings are already there, tucked into the words, and I’m just finally hearing them properly. Certain lines stand out in a way I can’t explain away. It’s not like I sat there wanting to find a message and then forced one into the song.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I understand why she says that. If I were listening to someone else say this, maybe I’d think it sounded strange too. But knowing it sounds odd doesn’t make it feel any less real to me. I still know what I heard in those words. So could my mind be part of it? Maybe, in some way. But I don’t believe it’s only my mind. I’m certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings in them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the warnings you hear in the ballads are not real?\n\nPatient: I suppose if I’m being fair, I can say there’s a possibility. I mean, I know how it sounds when I describe it. I’m not deaf to that. If someone else told me, “There are warnings hidden in these old ballads,” I might wonder if grief was making them hear things in a certain way.\n\nBut for me, sitting there with those songs my brother kept, listening to the words over and over… it doesn’t feel unreal. It doesn’t feel like I’m making it up. Certain lines just hit differently, like they’re not only lyrics anymore, like they were meant to be noticed by me. That’s the part I can’t get away from.\n\nMy wife says I’m reading too much into it, and I try to respect that because she’s not trying to hurt me. She’s worried, I think. But even when she says that, I still know what I heard in those words. So yes, maybe there’s some small possibility I’m wrong, but I don’t really believe the warnings are not real. I’m still pretty certain those ballads aren’t just songs. They’ve got warnings tucked inside them for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings in the ballads would still be there if you weren't listening to them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they would. That’s the strange part, I suppose. It doesn’t feel like the warnings only exist because I happen to be listening at that moment, like I’m creating them while the song plays. It feels more like they’re already in there, tucked into the ballads, whether I have them on or not.\n\nI know that probably sounds odd. I can hear myself saying it, and I know my wife would say, “You’re reading too much into it.” She has said that. But to me, those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs anymore. The words are carrying something. When I listen, I’m not putting the warning there — I’m noticing what’s already there.\n\nSo if I wasn’t listening, I suppose I wouldn’t be hearing them right then, obviously. But that doesn’t mean the warnings disappear. They’d still be in the songs, waiting, in a way. And when I go back and play them again, certain lines catch the same way, like they were meant for me to hear. That’s why it’s hard for me to dismiss it. I still know what I heard in those words.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the warnings in the ballads would still make sense to you if your brother was alive and told you there were no hidden messages in them?\n\nPatient: If my brother were alive and he sat right in front of me and said, “No, there are no hidden messages in those songs,” I’d want to believe him. I really would. He knew those ballads better than anyone, and they were his, in a way. I’d listen to him. I wouldn’t just brush him off.\n\nBut… I don’t know that it would make the warnings stop making sense to me. That’s the hard part. Because what I hear in them doesn’t feel like something I decided on because he died, or because someone told me to look for it. It feels like the words themselves are carrying it. Like the warnings are tucked in there, whether anyone else admits it or not.\n\nMaybe I’d think he was trying to protect me, or maybe he didn’t hear them the same way I do. I know that sounds odd. I can hear that it sounds odd even as I’m saying it. My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and maybe my brother would say the same thing if he were here. But even then… I’d still know what I heard in those words.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it would shake me some if he said that. I’d be upset, probably confused. But I don’t think it would make me believe the ballads are just songs. Not completely. I’m certain there are warnings in them for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think… yes and no. If they didn’t know my brother, or what happened, maybe they wouldn’t catch it the same way I do. They might just hear an old ballad and think it’s sad, or pretty, or strange, and then move on. I understand that. I know not everybody would sit with the words the way I have.

But that doesn’t mean the warnings aren’t there. That’s the part I keep trying to explain. It’s not like I’m inventing them out of nowhere. They’re tucked into the words, into certain lines, the way the songs turn. I know it sounds odd, and I know my wife says I’m reading too much into it, but when I hear them, I know what I heard.

Maybe someone else could hear them if they listened carefully enough. Maybe they wouldn’t need to know my brother’s whole story, but they’d have to be paying attention in the right way. Still, I do think they’re meant for me more than for just anyone. Those were his ballads, and when I listen now, they’re not just songs. They’ve got warnings in them, and I can’t make myself believe otherwise.
